# Family-income IGE vs earnings IGE: by race, sex and age, and why they differ

Generated by `34.IncomeVsEarningsIGE.py` (2026-10-17); full definitions in its docstring; every estimate in `output/income_vs_earnings_ige.csv`. Every cell: coefficient (s.e.; p-value), *** p<0.01, ** p<0.05, * p<0.1. S.e. clustered by 1968 family (NLSY: first-round household). Within each table every cell is its own regression and the joint covariance is that of the stacked, fully interacted regression, so the '(1) minus (k)' columns (same children, two outcomes), the White - Black rows and the tests across bands are valid tests. Race = parental household head's race; Black / White log IGEs unweighted (as `6.IGEMain.py`), Pooled = all races weighted (child-year core longitudinal weight); ranks weighted throughout. 'Mean >= 35 minus < 35' = mean over the bands 35-39 ... 50-55 minus the mean over 25-29 and 30-34 (NLSY97: 35-39, 40-44 vs 25-29, 30-34). Own earnings in logs are positive years only; ranks and band means keep zeros. Lee-Solon s.e. here are clustered by 1968 family (6.IGEMain.py clusters by child), so they are slightly larger than in `ige_main.md`; the point estimates are identical.

## 0. Headline

**Answer to the PI.** For SONS the family-income IGE and the own-earnings IGE are close within each race (differences of 0.00-0.05 log points at 40, in all three data sets), so the racial gap is about as large in earnings as in family income; with age the sons' EARNINGS gap opens somewhat faster, because other income (transfers, other household members) and, a little, the spouse cushion Black sons' family income relative to their earnings. For DAUGHTERS the two differ a lot, and the difference is racial and grows with age: White daughters' family income is far more persistent than their own earnings, Black daughters' is not, and the gap that opens after 30 in daughters' family income is a gap in their husbands' earnings given marriage (roughly 55-65% of the spouse channel) and in marriage itself (35-45%), not in their own earnings.

- **Sons at 40 (PSID, Lee-Solon).** Family income: Black 0.268*** (0.047; p=<0.001), White 0.473*** (0.038; p=<0.001), gap 0.204*** (0.061; p=<0.001). Own earnings: Black 0.220*** (0.042; p=<0.001), White 0.464*** (0.038; p=<0.001), gap 0.244*** (0.057; p=<0.001). Family minus earnings IGE: Black 0.048** (0.024; p=0.047), White 0.009 (0.025; p=0.734); difference in the racial gap -0.039 (0.035; p=0.258).
- **Daughters at 40.** Family income: Black 0.291*** (0.037; p=<0.001), White 0.453*** (0.036; p=<0.001), gap 0.162*** (0.052; p=0.002). Own earnings: Black 0.277*** (0.034; p=<0.001), White 0.311*** (0.042; p=<0.001), gap 0.035 (0.054; p=0.522). Family minus earnings: White 0.142*** (0.041; p=<0.001), Black 0.015 (0.025; p=0.561); difference in the racial gap 0.127*** (0.048; p=0.008). Their husbands' earnings: Black 0.171*** (0.045; p=<0.001), White 0.370*** (0.038; p=<0.001) (gap 0.199*** (0.058; p=<0.001)).
- **By age (PSID bands).** Sons: the gap in the family-income IGE goes -0.016 (25-29) -> 0.244 (40-44), in own earnings -0.041 -> 0.294; the change (>= 35 minus < 35) is 0.150** (0.060; p=0.013) vs 0.223*** (0.055; p=<0.001), difference -0.074* (0.041; p=0.070) (common child-years: -0.081*** (0.030; p=0.007)). Daughters: family income gap -0.003 -> 0.445 (50-55), change 0.223*** (0.046; p=<0.001); own earnings change -0.004 (0.049; p=0.941); difference 0.227*** (0.050; p=<0.001), trend of the difference 0.132*** (0.034; p=<0.001) per 10 years.
- **Channels (exact log split, common sample).** Daughters at 40: gap 0.154*** (0.048; p=0.001) = own earnings 0.031 (0.055; p=0.567) + spouse 0.115*** (0.043; p=0.008) + other income 0.008 (0.019; p=0.698) (spouse = 75%). Rise with age (>= 35 minus < 35): 0.159*** (0.046; p=<0.001) = own -0.006 (0.050; p=0.902) + spouse 0.175*** (0.047; p=<0.001) + other -0.009 (0.021; p=0.662); the spouse part splits into marriage 0.062* (0.032; p=0.052) and the husband's earnings given marriage 0.118*** (0.034; p=<0.001). Sons at 40: gap 0.197*** (0.056; p=<0.001) = own 0.244*** (0.057; p=<0.001) + spouse -0.014 (0.024; p=0.572) + other -0.033** (0.016; p=0.034); rise 0.140*** (0.054; p=0.010) = own 0.221*** (0.055; p=<0.001) + spouse -0.023 (0.027; p=0.390) + other -0.058*** (0.017; p=<0.001). The order-free LEVEL split agrees (Section 3d).
- **Marriage timing.** At 25-29, White children from richer families are less often partnered (gradient of partnered in ln parental income, common sample: White daughters -0.059*** (0.016; p=<0.001), White sons -0.106*** (0.016; p=<0.001); Black daughters 0.001 (0.018; p=0.964), Black sons 0.021 (0.019; p=0.287)); by 35-39 the White gradients are 0.036* (0.020; p=0.074) and 0.034* (0.019; p=0.066). Later marriage of advantaged White children holds down their family-income IGE in the late twenties (daughters' spouse channel gap at 25-29: -0.134*** (0.035; p=<0.001)), which is part of why the racial gap in family income starts near zero.
- **Parental measure.** With parents' labor income instead of total family income, the sons' racial gap at 40 shrinks in family income (0.101* (0.052; p=0.053)) but not in earnings (0.181*** (0.050; p=<0.001)); with the father's earnings (father-present sons): 0.077 (0.085; p=0.364) vs 0.163** (0.076; p=0.031). Black sons' family income is more tied to parental labor income than their earnings are (0.072*** (0.020; p=<0.001)), White sons' is not (-0.008 (0.020; p=0.680)). The earnings gap is robust to the parental measure; the family-income gap is not (EVIDENCE 1.31).
- **General (pooled) findings.** Sons: family-income and earnings IGEs differ by at most ~0.05 at any age (pooled difference at 40 0.030 (0.020; p=0.137); largest at 30-34, 0.048*** (0.014; p=<0.001)); both rise with age. Daughters: the family-income IGE exceeds the earnings IGE by 0.213*** (0.034; p=<0.001) at 40 and the difference grows from 0.020 at 25-29 to 0.261 at 50-55, because the family-income IGE rises while the White daughters' own-earnings IGE FALLS after 30 (change -0.065* (0.035; p=0.062)). Assortative mating is strong: the elasticity of a daughter's husband's earnings to her parents' income at 40 is 0.362*** (0.035; p=<0.001), larger than of her own earnings (0.301*** (0.031; p=<0.001)); sons' wives: 0.199*** (0.044; p=<0.001), and it declines with the son's age (0.302 at 25-29, 0.146 at 45-49). Couple earnings carry essentially all of family-income persistence (family minus couple-earnings IGE at 40: sons 0.003 (0.014; p=0.834), daughters 0.026* (0.016; p=0.097)).
- **Zeros.** Employment gradients are small (sons pooled 0.043*** (0.008; p=<0.001) per log point at 40) and do not differ by race for sons; Black daughters' employment is MORE tied to parental income than White daughters' at 30-39 (gap at 35-39 -0.059*** (0.021; p=0.005)). Keeping zero years in the sons' band-mean earnings makes the opening of the racial gap, if anything, larger (0.232*** (0.068; p=<0.001) with zeros vs 0.191*** (0.058; p=<0.001) without; difference 0.042 (0.031; p=0.185)).
- **Ranks.** National rank-rank slopes show the same SEX pattern (pooled, 35-44: sons family 0.431*** (0.023; p=<0.001) vs earnings 0.363*** (0.021; p=<0.001); daughters 0.445*** (0.022; p=<0.001) vs 0.227*** (0.021; p=<0.001)) and the daughters' racial difference (ryf - rye gap 0.156*** (0.046; p=<0.001)), but no racial divergence with age for sons in either outcome (Paper B: ranks are flat by race).
- **NLSY replication.** Sons' family-income and earnings IGEs are close in both NLSYs (family minus earnings, White sons, NLSY79 at 40: 0.005 (0.023; p=0.820); NLSY97 at 35: 0.011 (0.018; p=0.548)). Daughters' family-income IGE exceeds their earnings IGE and increasingly so with age (pooled change of the difference: NLSY79 0.144*** (0.029; p=<0.001), NLSY97 0.062*** (0.023; p=0.006)); the racial difference in it opens with age in the NLSY97 (0.102** (0.048; p=0.034)) and, not significantly, in the NLSY79 (0.071 (0.052; p=0.169)).

## 1. The grid at 40: child outcome x parental measure (PSID)

Each cell is a separate regression of the column outcome on the row's ln parental measure (Lee-Solon: child-years 25-65, quartics in parental age and (age-40), parental measure x age quartic; IGE at 40). Parental measures are means over child ages 13-19 (>= 3 years). Father-present = male head at 16; the 'father-present' row repeats the benchmark on that sample so rows 2 and 3 compare like with like. Spouse earnings: partnered child-years with the spouse's earnings > 0. Columns (5)-(7) test family income minus the other outcome, same parental measure, same children. N rows: child-years / children for the benchmark parental measure and for the father's-earnings measure.

## 2. By age (PSID): family income vs own earnings

Child-year regressions within each band: ln y on ln parental family income (13-19 mean), quartic in parental age, single-year age dummies. Column (4) is test (a): family-income IGE minus earnings IGE within race (rows Black, White, Pooled) and test (b): the racial gap in the family-income IGE minus the racial gap in the earnings IGE (row White - Black). The test rows of column (4) are test (c): does the age slope of the gap differ between the two outcomes. Column (3), employment, is an LPM (Section 4).

### 2c. Household labor earnings and the spouse

ln couple earnings = ln(own + spouse earnings), all child-years with a positive sum; ln spouse earnings: partnered child-years with positive spouse earnings (assortative mating, Chadwick-Solon). (1) minus (2) measures how much other income adds to persistence; (1) minus (3) compares the child's family income with the spouse's earnings.

### 2d. Ranks by age (PSID)

Rank-rank slopes: band means (>= 2 years) of the child's outcome ranked within birth year and band among all children; parent rank = parental family income (13-19 mean) within birth year; weighted. Own earnings rank (2) keeps zero years in the band mean; (3) averages positive years only (>= 2).

## 3. Why they differ: channels of the family-income IGE (PSID)

Common sample: child-years with family income in bounds, positive own earnings and known spouse earnings (0 if unpartnered). ln Y = ln E_own + ln[(E_own+E_sp)/E_own] + ln[Y/(E_own+E_sp)], so column (1) = (2) + (3) + (4) exactly in every row (checked in the code). Rows 'Lee-Solon IGE at 40' use the age-interacted design on child-years 25-65; band rows the child-year band design. Note that the split is sequential (own earnings first): a child who earns more has a smaller spouse term for the same spouse; the level split in 3d has no ordering.

### 3b. Marriage vs the spouse's earnings given marriage

The spouse term s = ln[(E_own+E_sp)/E_own] is 0 for unpartnered child-years, so beta_s = d E[s]/d ln y_p ~ mean(s | partnered) x beta_partnered + share partnered x beta_(s | partnered). (1) partnered LPM; (2) spouse term among the partnered; (3) marriage part = mean(s|m) x (1); (4) conditional part = share partnered x (2); (5) remainder = beta_s - (3) - (4) (means treated as fixed). (3)-(5) are linear combinations, not separate regressions.

### 3d. Level split (order-free, zeros kept)

Each component divided by the race x sex x single-year-of-age mean family income (pooled rows: the all-race weighted mean); slopes on ln parental income. (1) = (2) + (3) + (4) exactly; (1) is the semi-elasticity of mean family income (a 'level IGE'), not the log IGE. Sample: family income in bounds, own and spouse earnings known and <= $150,000 of 1967 (own earnings may be zero).

|---|---|---|---|---|
| 25-29 | 0.095 | 0.030 | 0.224 | 0.164 |
| 30-34 | 0.115 | 0.037 | 0.216 | 0.182 |

| 45-49 | 0.160 | 0.074 | 0.224 | 0.151 |
| 50-55 | 0.229 | 0.110 | 0.266 | 0.174 |

ln of the band mean of own earnings (one row per child and band, >= 2 years; A quartic + mean age in the band): (1) zero years averaged in (children with a band mean below $100 of 1967 dropped), (2) mean over positive years only (>= 2). Column (3) tests whether including zeros changes the IGE and the racial gap. Rank versions (zeros kept vs positive years only) are columns (2)-(3) of the tables in 2d.

## 5. NLSY79 and NLSY97 replication

NLSY79: born 1960-64, at home in 1979; parental income = mean family income 1978-80 (26.IGEByAge.py frame), ages 25-55; pooled = Black + White (the file's two groups) weighted by wt79. NLSY97: born 1980-84; parental income = 1996 gross household income (one year, parent report; 29.NLSY97AgeProfile.py frame), ages 25-44; 'White' = non-Black non-Hispanic; pooled = all races weighted by the round weight. No spouse earnings in these extracts. Common = child-years with both outcomes.

## 6. Caveats

- Spouse = the partner in the child's PSID family unit (married or cohabiting); 'other income' is a residual (family income minus the two labor incomes) and so includes transfers, asset income, other family members' income and business / farm income outside labor income; it is negative in ~4% of head-years (business losses). In the 1976-78 waves the wife's labor income is wages only (1.PSID.py).
- The father's-earnings measure exists only for children with a male head at 16 (about half of Black and nearly nine in ten White children, `24.IGEDecomposition.py`); compare it with the 'father-present' row, not with the full-sample benchmark. Here the father's earnings are the head's labor income in bounds, without 24's hours / wage validity screen, so the estimates differ slightly from `ige_decomposition.md`.
- The log decomposition is sequential and conditions on positive own earnings (it drops non-working daughters); the level split keeps zeros and has no ordering. The marriage split treats the cell means as fixed; its remainder is small in every cell.
- Black rank-rank slopes are imprecise (weighted, so the SEO over-sample counts for less); late bands (45-55) hold few children and early birth cohorts. NLSY97 parental income is a single year (attenuates both IGEs equally; it does not affect the within-race comparison of outcomes).
